What brings you in today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I don’t care about anything anymore—not my art classes, board-game nights, hiking, or even getting back to the soccer team, and I just sit in my room while everything slips away.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... well, I guess I'm here because I just don't care about anything anymore. And I mean absolutely anything. It’s like someone just flipped a switch and drained all the color and the point out of my life, you know? I used to do so much, I really did. I had my art classes—I took a few at the community college and I was actually working on this really big charcoal piece, a landscape, but now the sketchbook is just sitting on my desk gathering dust and I can't even bring myself to look at it, let alone pick up a pencil. 

And it's not just the art, it's everything. I used to go to these board-game nights every Thursday with a group of people I've known for years, we'd play strategy games for hours, but I haven't been in weeks. I can't even fathom sitting there and pretending to care about rolling dice or talking to anyone. Same with hiking... I used to love getting out on the trails, just feeling the air, and I was even thinking about getting back on the recreational soccer team I used to play for. I was a decent midfielder back in the day, and I thought it would be good for me. But now? Now I don't even want to tie my shoes. 

I just sit in my room. That's all I do. I sit in my room, usually just on the edge of the bed or in the chair by the window, and I watch the hours go by. It’s like I’m watching my whole life just slip away, just completely slip right through my fingers, and the worst part is I don't even have the energy to care that it's slipping. I just sit there.
=====================================

I used to go to these board-game nights every Thursday with a group of people I've known for years, we'd play strategy games for hours, but I haven't been in weeks. I can't even fathom sitting there and pretending to care about rolling dice or talking to anyone. Same with hiking... I used to love getting out on the trails, just feeling the air, and I was even thinking about getting back on the recreational soccer team I used to play for. I was a decent midfielder back in the day, and I thought it would be good for me. But now? Now I don't even want to tie my shoes. \n\nI just sit in my room. That's all I do. I sit in my room, usually just on the edge of the bed or in the chair by the window, and I watch the hours go by. It’s like I’m watching my whole life just slip away, just completely slip right through my fingers, and the worst part is I don't even have the energy to care that it's slipping. I just sit there.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of killing yourself?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Death_Suicide\nmanifestation: \"I keep having thoughts that I don’t want to wake up anymore, and when my friend found me I had been saying out loud that I couldn’t keep living like this.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... well, yes. I guess I have. It's not like I have this elaborate plan mapped out or anything, but... I just, every night when I finally lay down, I just close my eyes and I really, truly hope that I just don't wake up anymore. It would just be so much easier, you know? Just to drift off and not have to face the morning, because waking up is the hardest part. You open your eyes and you realize you have to do another whole day of just... nothing. Just sitting there with this heavy, crushing weight on my chest. So I keep having these thoughts that I just want it to end in my sleep, that I just don't want to wake up ever again. 

And it actually really scared my friend a few days ago. She's one of the people from that board game group I mentioned, she's always been the one who checks in on me the most. She came over because I hadn't been answering my phone for a while, and she still had a spare key from a couple of years ago. I didn't even hear her unlock the door or come down the hall. When she found me in my room, I was just sitting there in the dark, and I didn't even realize I was doing it, but I was actually saying it out loud. She told me she heard me before she even opened the bedroom door. I was just sitting there saying, over and over, that I couldn't keep living like this. I just kept saying it to the empty room. Because it's true, I just... I can't keep living like this. It's too much. I'm just so tired.

I mean... I've definitely had times where I felt down, you know? Like, everyone gets sad sometimes, and I remember a few years ago when my cat passed away, I was really upset for a couple of weeks. But nothing like this. This is entirely different. It's like comparing a paper cut to a broken leg. 

This whole... whatever this is, this heavy, suffocating feeling, it really started to settle in and just become constant around the summer of last year. I remember it was getting really hot out, and usually I love the summer, usually I'd be planning trips to the lake or trying to get a tan on my balcony, but I just... I stopped caring. The heat just made me feel more tired, and the sun felt almost oppressive, if that makes sense. And it just never lifted. The leaves changed, it got cold, the holidays came and went, and I felt nothing. And now it's been... what, a year and four months? Yeah, about a year and four months of just this endless, gray nothingness. It hasn't let up for a single day since that summer. Every single morning for sixteen months, just waking up and feeling like I'm already exhausted.

Because it's true, I just... I can't keep living like this. It's too much. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I mean... I've definitely had times where I felt down, you know? Like, everyone gets sad sometimes, and I remember a few years ago when my cat passed away, I was really upset for a couple of weeks. But nothing like this. This is entirely different. It's like comparing a paper cut to a broken leg. \n\nThis whole... whatever this is, this heavy, suffocating feeling, it really started to settle in and just become constant around the summer of last year. I remember it was getting really hot out, and usually I love the summer, usually I'd be planning trips to the lake or trying to get a tan on my balcony, but I just... I stopped caring. The heat just made me feel more tired, and the sun felt almost oppressive, if that makes sense. And it just never lifted. The leaves changed, it got cold, the holidays came and went, and I felt nothing. And now it's been... what, a year and four months? Yeah, about a year and four months of just this endless, gray nothingness. It hasn't let up for a single day since that summer. Every single morning for sixteen months, just waking up and feeling like I'm already exhausted.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas this depressed feeling lasted continuously for two years or longer?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressive Episode Duration\nmanifestation: \"The depression became constant around the following summer, and it has lasted about 1 year and 4 months.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, not two years. Like I was just saying, it really started last summer. I mean, I know time kind of blurs together when every day feels exactly the same, just this endless loop of waking up tired and going to bed hoping you don't wake up again, but I'm pretty sure about the timeline. It was the summer of last year. I remember because I had bought this new swimsuit, this really pretty navy blue one with little white anchors on it, and I was so excited to wear it to the lake with my friends. We usually do this big Fourth of July weekend thing, rent a cabin, the whole deal. But by the time the trip rolled around, I couldn't even bring myself to pack a bag. I just left the swimsuit in the shopping bag on my dresser. It's actually still sitting there, in the exact same spot. 

So, yeah, it started then, and it's just been constant ever since. I've been counting the months in my head, trying to figure out when it's going to end, or if it ever will. It's been about a year and four months now. Sixteen months of just... this. Sixteen months of staring at that shopping bag on my dresser and not caring that it's there. So, no, not two years. Just a year and four months. But honestly, it feels like it's been a lifetime. It feels like I've never felt any other way, even though I know logically that I used to. But it's just been since last summer.

Well, when you say a major life change... I mean, yes. My divorce. I know I just said a minute ago that the really heavy, constant part started last summer with the swimsuit and everything, but... if I'm being completely honest with myself, and really tracing it back to the absolute beginning? My ex-husband and I, we had been having issues for a while, just the usual things at first, arguing about bills or who was doing more around the apartment, but then it just completely fell apart. He moved out, and suddenly I was just sitting in this half-empty place because he took the good couch and the TV, and I just had this overwhelming sense of... I don't even know, just total failure, I guess. The paperwork was an absolute nightmare, and I actually had to drop my classes—I was taking a few courses at the community college, trying to finally finish my degree, but I just couldn't focus on biology or English lit when my whole life was collapsing around me. 

So, yeah, the divorce was about 1 year and 11 months ago, and this whole dark cloud really started forming right after that. I think last summer is just when it got so incredibly bad that I completely stopped functioning and couldn't even pack a bag for the lake, but the actual start of it? The beginning of this whole nightmare? That was definitely right after the divorce, about a year and eleven months ago. It's just been this slow, agonizing slide downhill ever since he packed up his car and drove away, and I just haven't been able to climb back up.

this.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: For much of this past year, the low mood and the voices have been happening at the same time; I would be lying in bed feeling hopeless while also hearing that woman telling me I ruined everything.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: for weeks.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve stopped taking freelance art-education work, missed community events and soccer, and I need my friend checking on me because I’m not reliably eating, showering, or keeping myself safe.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 11 months\n  manifestation: This Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 4 months\n  manifestation: The depression became constant around the following summer, and it has lasted about 1 year and 4 months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I mean... I've definitely had times where I felt down, you know? Like, everyone gets sad sometimes, and I remember a few years ago when my cat passed away, I was really upset for a couple of weeks. But nothing like this. This is entirely different. It's like comparing a paper cut to a broken leg. \n\nThis whole... whatever this is, this heavy, suffocating feeling, it really started to settle in and just become constant around the summer of last year. I remember it was getting really hot out, and usually I love the summer, usually I'd be planning trips to the lake or trying to get a tan on my balcony, but I just... I stopped caring. The heat just made me feel more tired, and the sun felt almost oppressive, if that makes sense. And it just never lifted. The leaves changed, it got cold, the holidays came and went, and I felt nothing. And now it's been... what, a year and four months? Yeah, about a year and four months of just this endless, gray nothingness. It hasn't let up for a single day since that summer. Every single morning for sixteen months, just waking up and feeling like I'm already exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Has this depressed feeling lasted continuously for two years or longer?\n\nPatient: No, no, not two years. Like I was just saying, it really started last summer. I mean, I know time kind of blurs together when every day feels exactly the same, just this endless loop of waking up tired and going to bed hoping you don't wake up again, but I'm pretty sure about the timeline. It was the summer of last year. I remember because I had bought this new swimsuit, this really pretty navy blue one with little white anchors on it, and I was so excited to wear it to the lake with my friends. We usually do this big Fourth of July weekend thing, rent a cabin, the whole deal. But by the time the trip rolled around, I couldn't even bring myself to pack a bag. I just left the swimsuit in the shopping bag on my dresser. It's actually still sitting there, in the exact same spot. \n\nSo, yeah, it started then, and it's just been constant ever since. Sixteen months of staring at that shopping bag on my dresser and not caring that it's there. So, no, not two years. Just a year and four months. But honestly, it feels like it's been a lifetime. It feels like I've never felt any other way, even though I know logically that I used to. But it's just been since last summer.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Well, when you say a major life change... I mean, yes. My divorce. I know I just said a minute ago that the really heavy, constant part started last summer with the swimsuit and everything, but... if I'm being completely honest with myself, and really tracing it back to the absolute beginning? It actually started shortly after the divorce. That was... wow, it's been about 1 year and 11 months now. Almost two years. God, time is just... it's so weird to keep track of now. \n\nMy ex-husband and I, we had been having issues for a while, just the usual things at first, arguing about bills or who was doing more around the apartment, but then it just completely fell apart. He moved out, and suddenly I was just sitting in this half-empty place because he took the good couch and the TV, and I just had this overwhelming sense of... I don't even know, just total failure, I guess. The paperwork was an absolute nightmare, and I actually had to drop my classes—I was taking a few courses at the community college, trying to finally finish my degree, but I just couldn't focus on biology or English lit when my whole life was collapsing around me. \n\nSo, yeah, the divorce was about 1 year and 11 months ago, and this whole dark cloud really started forming right after that. I think last summer is just when it got so incredibly bad that I completely stopped functioning and couldn't even pack a bag for the lake, but the actual start of it? The beginning of this whole nightmare? That was definitely right after the divorce, about a year and eleven months ago. It's just been this slow, agonizing slide downhill ever since he packed up his car and drove away, and I just haven't been able to climb back up.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these symptoms been going on continuously for 2 years or more?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. No, not quite two years, but almost. Like I was just saying, it's been about a year and eleven months. I know you just asked me that, and I said sixteen months before, but when I really sit here and think about it—really trace the timeline back to when things started feeling wrong, before they got completely unbearable last summer—it was right after the divorce. It's just... it's hard to explain how time feels right now. You lose track of whether it's Tuesday or Sunday, whether it's been a month or a year. But yes, to answer your question specifically, it has not been a full two years. It's been one year and eleven months of this continuous... just this continuous weight, ever since he left and the paperwork started and everything just fell apart. So, almost two years, but not quite. I guess next month will make it exactly two years, which is just a horrifying thought, honestly, that I could feel this way for that long without it breaking me completely. Or maybe it already has broken me, I don't know. But no, officially, one year and eleven months.

She came over because I hadn't been answering my phone for a while, and she still had a spare key from a couple of years ago. I didn't even hear her unlock the door or come down the hall. When she found me in my room, I was just sitting there in the dark, and I didn't even realize I was doing it, but I was actually saying it out loud. She told me she heard me before she even opened the bedroom door. I was just sitting there saying, over and over, that I couldn't keep living like this. I just kept saying it to the empty room. Because it's true, I just... I can't keep living like this. It's too much. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I mean... I've definitely had times where I felt down, you know? Like, everyone gets sad sometimes, and I remember a few years ago when my cat passed away, I was really upset for a couple of weeks. But nothing like this. This is entirely different. It's like comparing a paper cut to a broken leg. \n\nThis whole... whatever this is, this heavy, suffocating feeling, it really started to settle in and just become constant around the summer of last year. I remember it was getting really hot out, and usually I love the summer, usually I'd be planning trips to the lake or trying to get a tan on my balcony, but I just... I stopped caring. The heat just made me feel more tired, and the sun felt almost oppressive, if that makes sense. And it just never lifted. The leaves changed, it got cold, the holidays came and went, and I felt nothing. And now it's been... what, a year and four months? Yeah, about a year and four months of just this endless, gray nothingness. It hasn't let up for a single day since that summer. Every single morning for sixteen months, just waking up and feeling like I'm already exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Has this depressed feeling lasted continuously for two years or longer?\n\nPatient: No, no, not two years. Like I was just saying, it really started last summer. I mean, I know time kind of blurs together when every day feels exactly the same, just this endless loop of waking up tired and going to bed hoping you don't wake up again, but I'm pretty sure about the timeline. It was the summer of last year. I remember because I had bought this new swimsuit, this really pretty navy blue one with little white anchors on it, and I was so excited to wear it to the lake with my friends. I just left the swimsuit in the shopping bag on my dresser. It's actually still sitting there, in the exact same spot. \n\nSo, yeah, it started then, and it's just been constant ever since. Like I was just saying, it's been about a year and eleven months. I guess next month will make it exactly two years, which is just a horrifying thought, honestly, that I could feel this way for that long without it breaking me completely. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, it's hard to just pin it all on one thing, you know? The divorce was definitely the catalyst, I guess you could say, the thing that really kicked the bottom out from under me. But... it's not just the divorce itself, it's everything that came with it, and everything that followed. It's like the divorce was the earthquake, and everything else since then has just been this massive tsunami washing away whatever was left of my life. 

I mean, I think about it a lot, obviously, because I have nothing else to do but sit around and think. And sure, losing my marriage was devastating. We had been together since I was twenty-five, you know? We had all these plans. We were going to buy a house, maybe start a family once I finished my degree. And then he just... decided he didn't want that anymore. He didn't want me anymore. So, yeah, that hurts. It hurts a lot. 

But it's also the fact that I had to drop out of school because of it. I was doing so well in my classes, finally getting my life on track, and then I just couldn't concentrate. I couldn't even read a single page of my textbooks without crying or just staring blankly at the words until they looked like a foreign language. And because I dropped out, I felt like a failure. And because I felt like a failure, I didn't want to see my friends. I didn't want to go to the lake last summer. I didn't want to do anything. 

So, is it mainly because of the divorce? I don't know. Maybe at first it was. But now... now it just feels like it's taken on a life of its own. It's like the sadness started with him leaving, but then it just grew and grew until it swallowed everything else. It's not just about him anymore, or the marriage ending. It's about me, and how completely broken I feel, and how I don't know how to fix it. It's the empty apartment, and the unfinished degree, and the swimsuit sitting on my dresser, and just... the absolute hopelessness of it all. So, I guess it started with the divorce, but it feels like it's about so much more than that now. It just feels like it's about everything.

It’s like I’m watching my whole life just slip away, just completely slip right through my fingers, and the worst part is I don't even have the energy to care that it's slipping. I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of killing yourself?\n\nPatient: I... well, yes. I guess I have. It's not like I have this elaborate plan mapped out or anything, but... I just, every night when I finally lay down, I just close my eyes and I really, truly hope that I just don't wake up anymore. It would just be so much easier, you know? Just to drift off and not have to face the morning, because waking up is the hardest part. You open your eyes and you realize you have to do another whole day of just... nothing. Just sitting there with this heavy, crushing weight on my chest. So I keep having these thoughts that I just want it to end in my sleep, that I just don't want to wake up ever again. \n\nAnd it actually really scared my friend a few days ago. She's one of the people from that board game group I mentioned, she's always been the one who checks in on me the most. She came over because I hadn't been answering my phone for a while, and she still had a spare key from a couple of years ago. I didn't even hear her unlock the door or come down the hall. When she found me in my room, I was just sitting there in the dark, and I didn't even realize I was doing it, but I was actually saying it out loud. She told me she heard me before she even opened the bedroom door. I was just sitting there saying, over and over, that I couldn't keep living like this. I just kept saying it to the empty room. Because it's true, I just... I can't keep living like this. It's too much. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I mean... I've definitely had times where I felt down, you know? Like, everyone gets sad sometimes, and I remember a few years ago when my cat passed away, I was really upset for a couple of weeks. But nothing like this. This is entirely different. It's like comparing a paper cut to a broken leg. \n\nThis whole... whatever this is, this heavy, suffocating feeling, it really started to settle in and just become constant around the summer of last year. I remember it was getting really hot out, and usually I love the summer, usually I'd be planning trips to the lake or trying to get a tan on my balcony, but I just... I stopped caring. The heat just made me feel more tired, and the sun felt almost oppressive, if that makes sense. And it just never lifted. The leaves changed, it got cold, the holidays came and went, and I felt nothing. And now it's been... what, a year and four months? Yeah, about a year and four months of just this endless, gray nothingness. It hasn't let up for a single day since that summer. Every single morning for sixteen months, just waking up and feeling like I'm already exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Has this depressed feeling lasted continuously for two years or longer?\n\nPatient: No, no, not two years. Like I was just saying, it really started last summer. I mean, I know time kind of blurs together when every day feels exactly the same, just this endless loop of waking up tired and going to bed hoping you don't wake up again, but I'm pretty sure about the timeline. It was the summer of last year. I remember because I had bought this new swimsuit, this really pretty navy blue one with little white anchors on it, and I was so excited to wear it to the lake with my friends. We usually do this big Fourth of July weekend thing, rent a cabin, the whole deal. But by the time the trip rolled around, I couldn't even bring myself to pack a bag. I just left the swimsuit in the shopping bag on my dresser. It's actually still sitting there, in the exact same spot. \n\nSo, yeah, it started then, and it's just been constant ever since. I've been counting the months in my head, trying to figure out when it's going to end, or if it ever will. It's been about a year and four months now. Sixteen months of just... this. Sixteen months of staring at that shopping bag on my dresser and not caring that it's there. So, no, not two years. Just a year and four months. But honestly, it feels like it's been a lifetime. It feels like I've never felt any other way, even though I know logically that I used to. But it's just been since last summer.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Well, when you say a major life change... I mean, yes. My divorce. I know I just said a minute ago that the really heavy, constant part started last summer with the swimsuit and everything, but... if I'm being completely honest with myself, and really tracing it back to the absolute beginning? It actually started shortly after the divorce. That was... wow, it's been about 1 year and 11 months now. Almost two years. God, time is just... it's so weird to keep track of now. \n\nMy ex-husband and I, we had been having issues for a while, just the usual things at first, arguing about bills or who was doing more around the apartment, but then it just completely fell apart. He moved out, and suddenly I was just sitting in this half-empty place because he took the good couch and the TV, and I just had this overwhelming sense of... I don't even know, just total failure, I guess. The paperwork was an absolute nightmare, and I actually had to drop my classes—I was taking a few courses at the community college, trying to finally finish my degree, but I just couldn't focus on biology or English lit when my whole life was collapsing around me. \n\nSo, yeah, the divorce was about 1 year and 11 months ago, and this whole dark cloud really started forming right after that. I think last summer is just when it got so incredibly bad that I completely stopped functioning and couldn't even pack a bag for the lake, but the actual start of it? The beginning of this whole nightmare? That was definitely right after the divorce, about a year and eleven months ago. It's just been this slow, agonizing slide downhill ever since he packed up his car and drove away, and I just haven't been able to climb back up.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been going on continuously for 2 years or more?\n\nPatient: No, not quite two years, but almost. Like I was just saying, it's been about a year and eleven months. I know you just asked me that, and I said sixteen months before, but when I really sit here and think about it—really trace the timeline back to when things started feeling wrong, before they got completely unbearable last summer—it was right after the divorce. And that was a year and eleven months ago. \n\nIt's just... it's hard to explain how time feels right now. When you're in it, when you're just waking up every single day and the air feels like it's made of lead, and you're just staring at the same walls where his pictures used to hang... the days just bleed into each other. You lose track of whether it's Tuesday or Sunday, whether it's been a month or a year. But yes, to answer your question specifically, it has not been a full two years. It's been one year and eleven months of this continuous... just this continuous weight, ever since he left and the paperwork started and everything just fell apart. So, almost two years, but not quite. I guess next month will make it exactly two years, which is just a horrifying thought, honestly, that I could feel this way for that long without it breaking me completely. Or maybe it already has broken me, I don't know. But no, officially, one year and eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this has all been mainly because of the divorce?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, it's hard to just pin it all on one thing, you know? The divorce was definitely the catalyst, I guess you could say, the thing that really kicked the bottom out from under me. But... it's not just the divorce itself, it's everything that came with it, and everything that followed. It's like the divorce was the earthquake, and everything else since then has just been this massive tsunami washing away whatever was left of my life. \n\nI mean, I think about it a lot, obviously, because I have nothing else to do but sit around and think. And sure, losing my marriage was devastating. We had been together since I was twenty-five, you know? We had all these plans. We were going to buy a house, maybe start a family once I finished my degree. And then he just... decided he didn't want that anymore. He didn't want me anymore. So, yeah, that hurts. It hurts a lot. \n\nBut it's also the fact that I had to drop out of school because of it. I was doing so well in my classes, finally getting my life on track, and then I just couldn't concentrate. I couldn't even read a single page of my textbooks without crying or just staring blankly at the words until they looked like a foreign language. And because I dropped out, I felt like a failure. And because I felt like a failure, I didn't want to see my friends. I didn't want to go to the lake last summer. I didn't want to do anything. \n\nSo, is it mainly because of the divorce? I don't know. Maybe at first it was. But now... now it just feels like it's taken on a life of its own. It's like the sadness started with him leaving, but then it just grew and grew until it swallowed everything else. It's not just about him anymore, or the marriage ending. It's about me, and how completely broken I feel, and how I don't know how to fix it. It's the empty apartment, and the unfinished degree, and the swimsuit sitting on my dresser, and just... the absolute hopelessness of it all. So, I guess it started with the divorce, but it feels like it's about so much more than that now. It just feels like it's about everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my sleep is just... it's a complete mess, honestly. It's not just waking up early, it's the whole night. The whole process of trying to sleep is just exhausting in itself. I mean, first of all, I can't even fall asleep because I just lie there for hours. I'll get into bed at a normal time, you know, like ten or eleven, hoping that maybe tonight will be the night I actually get some rest, but then my brain just won't shut off. And it's not just thinking about the divorce or the classes I dropped. It's this awful, creeping feeling. I end up getting out of bed, checking the locks on the front door, making sure the deadbolt is turned all the way. Then I'll check the windows. Then I'll go back to bed, but I'll just lie there, staring at the ceiling, listening to every single sound in the building. Every creak of the floorboards upstairs, every car driving by outside, I'm just listening and waiting, feeling so on edge. 

So by the time I actually manage to drift off, which is usually not until two or three in the morning, I'm already exhausted. But then, yes, to answer your question, I wake up way too early. Like, before the sun is even up. It'll be four-thirty or five in the morning, and my eyes just snap open. And I'm not just waking up calmly, I wake up literally shaking. My whole body is just trembling, and my heart is racing like I've been running a marathon in my sleep, though I can never remember what I was dreaming about. And once I'm awake like that, in the pitch dark, shaking and sweating, there is just absolutely no way I can get back to sleep. I try pulling the covers up, trying to get warm, trying to force myself back under, but it's impossible. I just lie there, shaking, waiting for the sun to come up, knowing that I have to face another whole day feeling completely drained before it even starts. So, yeah, I wake up way earlier than I should, and falling back asleep is just not an option. It's like my body is terrified of something, but I don't even know what it is.

I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of killing yourself?\n\nPatient: I... well, yes. I guess I have. It's not like I have this elaborate plan mapped out or anything, but... I just, every night when I finally lay down, I just close my eyes and I really, truly hope that I just don't wake up anymore. It would just be so much easier, you know? Just to drift off and not have to face the morning, because waking up is the hardest part. You open your eyes and you realize you have to do another whole day of just... nothing. Just sitting there with this heavy, crushing weight on my chest. So I keep having these thoughts that I just want it to end in my sleep, that I just don't want to wake up ever again. \n\nAnd it actually really scared my friend a few days ago. She's one of the people from that board game group I mentioned, she's always been the one who checks in on me the most. She came over because I hadn't been answering my phone for a while, and she still had a spare key from a couple of years ago. I didn't even hear her unlock the door or come down the hall. When she found me in my room, I was just sitting there in the dark, and I didn't even realize I was doing it, but I was actually saying it out loud. She told me she heard me before she even opened the bedroom door. I was just sitting there saying, over and over, that I couldn't keep living like this. I just kept saying it to the empty room. Because it's true, I just... I can't keep living like this. It's too much. I'm just so tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I mean... I've definitely had times where I felt down, you know? Like, everyone gets sad sometimes, and I remember a few years ago when my cat passed away, I was really upset for a couple of weeks. But nothing like this. This is entirely different. It's like comparing a paper cut to a broken leg. \n\nThis whole... whatever this is, this heavy, suffocating feeling, it really started to settle in and just become constant around the summer of last year. I remember it was getting really hot out, and usually I love the summer, usually I'd be planning trips to the lake or trying to get a tan on my balcony, but I just... I stopped caring. The heat just made me feel more tired, and the sun felt almost oppressive, if that makes sense. And it just never lifted. The leaves changed, it got cold, the holidays came and went, and I felt nothing. And now it's been... what, a year and four months? Yeah, about a year and four months of just this endless, gray nothingness. It hasn't let up for a single day since that summer. Every single morning for sixteen months, just waking up and feeling like I'm already exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Has this depressed feeling lasted continuously for two years or longer?\n\nPatient: No, no, not two years. Like I was just saying, it really started last summer. I mean, I know time kind of blurs together when every day feels exactly the same, just this endless loop of waking up tired and going to bed hoping you don't wake up again, but I'm pretty sure about the timeline. It was the summer of last year. I remember because I had bought this new swimsuit, this really pretty navy blue one with little white anchors on it, and I was so excited to wear it to the lake with my friends. We usually do this big Fourth of July weekend thing, rent a cabin, the whole deal. But by the time the trip rolled around, I couldn't even bring myself to pack a bag. I just left the swimsuit in the shopping bag on my dresser. It's actually still sitting there, in the exact same spot. \n\nSo, yeah, it started then, and it's just been constant ever since. I've been counting the months in my head, trying to figure out when it's going to end, or if it ever will. It's been about a year and four months now. Sixteen months of just... this. Sixteen months of staring at that shopping bag on my dresser and not caring that it's there. So, no, not two years. Just a year and four months. But honestly, it feels like it's been a lifetime. It feels like I've never felt any other way, even though I know logically that I used to. But it's just been since last summer.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Well, when you say a major life change... I mean, yes. My divorce. I know I just said a minute ago that the really heavy, constant part started last summer with the swimsuit and everything, but... if I'm being completely honest with myself, and really tracing it back to the absolute beginning? It actually started shortly after the divorce. That was... wow, it's been about 1 year and 11 months now. Almost two years. God, time is just... it's so weird to keep track of now. \n\nMy ex-husband and I, we had been having issues for a while, just the usual things at first, arguing about bills or who was doing more around the apartment, but then it just completely fell apart. He moved out, and suddenly I was just sitting in this half-empty place because he took the good couch and the TV, and I just had this overwhelming sense of... I don't even know, just total failure, I guess. The paperwork was an absolute nightmare, and I actually had to drop my classes—I was taking a few courses at the community college, trying to finally finish my degree, but I just couldn't focus on biology or English lit when my whole life was collapsing around me. \n\nSo, yeah, the divorce was about 1 year and 11 months ago, and this whole dark cloud really started forming right after that. I think last summer is just when it got so incredibly bad that I completely stopped functioning and couldn't even pack a bag for the lake, but the actual start of it? The beginning of this whole nightmare? That was definitely right after the divorce, about a year and eleven months ago. It's just been this slow, agonizing slide downhill ever since he packed up his car and drove away, and I just haven't been able to climb back up.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been going on continuously for 2 years or more?\n\nPatient: No, not quite two years, but almost. Like I was just saying, it's been about a year and eleven months. I know you just asked me that, and I said sixteen months before, but when I really sit here and think about it—really trace the timeline back to when things started feeling wrong, before they got completely unbearable last summer—it was right after the divorce. And that was a year and eleven months ago. \n\nIt's just... it's hard to explain how time feels right now. When you're in it, when you're just waking up every single day and the air feels like it's made of lead, and you're just staring at the same walls where his pictures used to hang... the days just bleed into each other. You lose track of whether it's Tuesday or Sunday, whether it's been a month or a year. But yes, to answer your question specifically, it has not been a full two years. It's been one year and eleven months of this continuous... just this continuous weight, ever since he left and the paperwork started and everything just fell apart. So, almost two years, but not quite. I guess next month will make it exactly two years, which is just a horrifying thought, honestly, that I could feel this way for that long without it breaking me completely. Or maybe it already has broken me, I don't know. But no, officially, one year and eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this has all been mainly because of the divorce?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, it's hard to just pin it all on one thing, you know? The divorce was definitely the catalyst, I guess you could say, the thing that really kicked the bottom out from under me. But... it's not just the divorce itself, it's everything that came with it, and everything that followed. It's like the divorce was the earthquake, and everything else since then has just been this massive tsunami washing away whatever was left of my life. \n\nI mean, I think about it a lot, obviously, because I have nothing else to do but sit around and think. And sure, losing my marriage was devastating. We had been together since I was twenty-five, you know? We had all these plans. We were going to buy a house, maybe start a family once I finished my degree. And then he just... decided he didn't want that anymore. He didn't want me anymore. So, yeah, that hurts. It hurts a lot. \n\nBut it's also the fact that I had to drop out of school because of it. I was doing so well in my classes, finally getting my life on track, and then I just couldn't concentrate. I couldn't even read a single page of my textbooks without crying or just staring blankly at the words until they looked like a foreign language. And because I dropped out, I felt like a failure. And because I felt like a failure, I didn't want to see my friends. I didn't want to go to the lake last summer. I didn't want to do anything. \n\nSo, is it mainly because of the divorce? I don't know. Maybe at first it was. But now... now it just feels like it's taken on a life of its own. It's like the sadness started with him leaving, but then it just grew and grew until it swallowed everything else. It's not just about him anymore, or the marriage ending. It's about me, and how completely broken I feel, and how I don't know how to fix it. It's the empty apartment, and the unfinished degree, and the swimsuit sitting on my dresser, and just... the absolute hopelessness of it all. So, I guess it started with the divorce, but it feels like it's about so much more than that now. It just feels like it's about everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been waking up much earlier than usual and then unable to fall back asleep?\n\nPatient: Well, my sleep is just... it's a complete mess, honestly. It's not just waking up early, it's the whole night. The whole process of trying to sleep is just exhausting in itself. I mean, first of all, I can't even fall asleep because I just lie there for hours. I'll get into bed at a normal time, you know, like ten or eleven, hoping that maybe tonight will be the night I actually get some rest, but then my brain just won't shut off. And it's not just thinking about the divorce or the classes I dropped. It's this awful, creeping feeling. I end up getting out of bed, checking the locks on the front door, making sure the deadbolt is turned all the way. Then I'll check the windows. Then I'll go back to bed, but I'll just lie there, staring at the ceiling, listening to every single sound in the building. Every creak of the floorboards upstairs, every car driving by outside, I'm just listening and waiting, feeling so on edge. \n\nSo by the time I actually manage to drift off, which is usually not until two or three in the morning, I'm already exhausted. But then, yes, to answer your question, I wake up way too early. Like, before the sun is even up. It'll be four-thirty or five in the morning, and my eyes just snap open. And I'm not just waking up calmly, I wake up literally shaking. My whole body is just trembling, and my heart is racing like I've been running a marathon in my sleep, though I can never remember what I was dreaming about. And once I'm awake like that, in the pitch dark, shaking and sweating, there is just absolutely no way I can get back to sleep. I try pulling the covers up, trying to get warm, trying to force myself back under, but it's impossible. I just lie there, shaking, waiting for the sun to come up, knowing that I have to face another whole day feeling completely drained before it even starts. So, yeah, I wake up way earlier than I should, and falling back asleep is just not an option. It's like my body is terrified of something, but I don't even know what it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. My whole body is just trembling, and my heart is racing like I've been running a marathon in my sleep, though I can never remember what I was dreaming about. And once I'm awake like that, in the pitch dark, shaking and sweating, there is just absolutely no way I can get back to sleep. I try pulling the covers up, trying to get warm, trying to force myself back under, but it's impossible. I just lie there, shaking, waiting for the sun to come up, knowing that I have to face another whole day feeling completely drained before it even starts. So, yeah, I wake up way earlier than I should, and falling back asleep is just not an option. It's like my body is terrified of something, but I don't even know what it is.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you wake up shaking and on edge, are those feelings mainly about the divorce and its aftermath?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know it sounds impossible, but I’m sure the people downstairs and some of my ex’s friends have been tracking when I leave, because the hallway lights blink right after I think about going to the store.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it’s… I mean, you would think so, right? Because of everything I just said about the paperwork and the empty apartment and how much it hurts. But actually, no. When I wake up like that, when my heart is just pounding out of my chest and I’m shivering under the covers… it’s not sadness about the marriage ending. It’s not about the divorce at all in those moments. It’s fear. It’s just this absolute, paralyzing terror about what’s happening to me right now, in this building.

Because—and I know it sounds impossible, I really do, I know how it sounds when I say it out loud—but I’m sure they are tracking me. The people downstairs, mostly. But also some of my ex’s friends. I think they’re coordinating with each other, keeping tabs on exactly when I leave the building, tracking every single move I make. 

And I wouldn't just say that without a reason, you know? I've been noticing it. It’s the lights in the hallway. I’ll just be sitting in my living room, or lying in bed, and I’ll just... I'll just *think* about going to the store. Like, I won't even pick up my keys, I won't put on my coat or say a single word out loud. I’ll just have the thought in my head, like, 'I should go get some milk.' And right after I think it, the hallway lights blink. I can see the flicker under the gap at the bottom of my front door. It happens right after I think about going out, every single time, like they have some kind of system set up to signal each other that I’m about to move, or like they can actually monitor what I'm planning to do before I even do it. 

So when I wake up at four in the morning shaking like a leaf, it’s because I know they’re out there. I know they’re downstairs, tracking me, and I can't even form a thought about leaving my own apartment without them knowing about it and flashing the lights. It's just so terrifying to be watched like that, to be trapped like that. So, no, it's not the divorce making me shake. It's them.

The leaves changed, it got cold, the holidays came and went, and I felt nothing. And now it's been... what, a year and four months? Sixteen months of staring at that shopping bag on my dresser and not caring that it's there. So, no, not two years. Just a year and four months. But honestly, it feels like it's been a lifetime. It feels like I've never felt any other way, even though I know logically that I used to. But it's just been since last summer.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Well, when you say a major life change... I mean, yes. My divorce. God, time is just... it's so weird to keep track of now. \n\nMy ex-husband and I, we had been having issues for a while, just the usual things at first, arguing about bills or who was doing more around the apartment, but then it just completely fell apart. And because I felt like a failure, I didn't want to see my friends. I didn't want to go to the lake last summer. I didn't want to do anything. \n\nSo, is it mainly because of the divorce? Or maybe it already has broken me, I don't know. But no, officially, one year and eleven months.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this has all been mainly because of the divorce?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, it's hard to just pin it all on one thing, you know? The divorce was definitely the catalyst, I guess you could say, the thing that really kicked the bottom out from under me. But... it's not just the divorce itself, it's everything that came with it, and everything that followed. It's like the divorce was the earthquake, and everything else since then has just been this massive tsunami washing away whatever was left of my life. \n\nI mean, I think about it a lot, obviously, because I have nothing else to do but sit around and think. And sure, losing my marriage was devastating. We had been together since I was twenty-five, you know? We had all these plans. We were going to buy a house, maybe start a family once I finished my degree. And then he just... decided he didn't want that anymore. He didn't want me anymore. So, yeah, that hurts. It hurts a lot. \n\nBut it's also the fact that I had to drop out of school because of it. I was doing so well in my classes, finally getting my life on track, and then I just couldn't concentrate. I couldn't even read a single page of my textbooks without crying or just staring blankly at the words until they looked like a foreign language. And because I dropped out, I felt like a failure. And because I felt like a failure, I didn't want to see my friends. I didn't want to go to the lake last summer. I didn't want to do anything. \n\nSo, is it mainly because of the divorce? I don't know. Maybe at first it was. But now... now it just feels like it's taken on a life of its own. It's like the sadness started with him leaving, but then it just grew and grew until it swallowed everything else. It's not just about him anymore, or the marriage ending. It's about me, and how completely broken I feel, and how I don't know how to fix it. It's the empty apartment, and the unfinished degree, and the swimsuit sitting on my dresser, and just... the absolute hopelessness of it all. So, I guess it started with the divorce, but it feels like it's about so much more than that now. It just feels like it's about everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been waking up much earlier than usual and then unable to fall back asleep?\n\nPatient: Well, my sleep is just... it's a complete mess, honestly. It's not just waking up early, it's the whole night. The whole process of trying to sleep is just exhausting in itself. I mean, first of all, I can't even fall asleep because I just lie there for hours. I'll get into bed at a normal time, you know, like ten or eleven, hoping that maybe tonight will be the night I actually get some rest, but then my brain just won't shut off. And it's not just thinking about the divorce or the classes I dropped. It's this awful, creeping feeling. I end up getting out of bed, checking the locks on the front door, making sure the deadbolt is turned all the way. Then I'll check the windows. Then I'll go back to bed, but I'll just lie there, staring at the ceiling, listening to every single sound in the building. Every creak of the floorboards upstairs, every car driving by outside, I'm just listening and waiting, feeling so on edge. \n\nSo by the time I actually manage to drift off, which is usually not until two or three in the morning, I'm already exhausted. But then, yes, to answer your question, I wake up way too early. Like, before the sun is even up. It'll be four-thirty or five in the morning, and my eyes just snap open. And I'm not just waking up calmly, I wake up literally shaking. My whole body is just trembling, and my heart is racing like I've been running a marathon in my sleep, though I can never remember what I was dreaming about. And once I'm awake like that, in the pitch dark, shaking and sweating, there is just absolutely no way I can get back to sleep. I try pulling the covers up, trying to get warm, trying to force myself back under, but it's impossible. I just lie there, shaking, waiting for the sun to come up, knowing that I have to face another whole day feeling completely drained before it even starts. So, yeah, I wake up way earlier than I should, and falling back asleep is just not an option. It's like my body is terrified of something, but I don't even know what it is.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up shaking and on edge, are those feelings mainly about the divorce and its aftermath?\n\nPatient: No, it’s… I mean, you would think so, right? Because of everything I just said about the paperwork and the empty apartment and how much it hurts. But actually, no. When I wake up like that, when my heart is just pounding out of my chest and I’m shivering under the covers… it’s not sadness about the marriage ending. It’s not about the divorce at all in those moments. It’s fear. It’s just this absolute, paralyzing terror about what’s happening to me right now, in this building.\n\nBecause—and I know it sounds impossible, I really do, I know how it sounds when I say it out loud—but I’m sure they are tracking me. The people downstairs, mostly. But also some of my ex’s friends. I think they’re coordinating with each other, keeping tabs on exactly when I leave the building, tracking every single move I make. \n\nAnd I wouldn't just say that without a reason, you know? I've been noticing it. It’s the lights in the hallway. I’ll just be sitting in my living room, or lying in bed, and I’ll just... I'll just *think* about going to the store. Like, I won't even pick up my keys, I won't put on my coat or say a single word out loud. I’ll just have the thought in my head, like, 'I should go get some milk.' And right after I think it, the hallway lights blink. I can see the flicker under the gap at the bottom of my front door. It happens right after I think about going out, every single time, like they have some kind of system set up to signal each other that I’m about to move, or like they can actually monitor what I'm planning to do before I even do it. \n\nSo when I wake up at four in the morning shaking like a leaf, it’s because I know they’re out there. I know they’re downstairs, tracking me, and I can't even form a thought about leaving my own apartment without them knowing about it and flashing the lights. It's just so terrifying to be watched like that, to be trapped like that. So, no, it's not the divorce making me shake. It's them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Well, no, not exactly. I mean, the really heavy, crushing sadness... the part where I couldn't get out of bed or even look at my coursework without completely breaking down... that actually came a bit later. There was this whole stretch of time before the worst of the depression really set in. 

During that time, I was actually still functioning, at least on the surface, you know? Like, I could still manage to cook sometimes. Nothing elaborate, maybe just boiling some pasta or making a piece of toast so I had something in my stomach, but I was getting up and doing it. And I was still answering my friends when they reached out. I might have just been texting them back to say I was busy or making up excuses for why I couldn't see them, but I was replying, I was still somewhat connected to the world in that way. I wasn't totally shut down yet.

But the thing is, even during that stretch, even when I was still doing those normal things and pushing through the days... the other stuff was already happening. It had been going on for weeks. I was already watching the gap under the front door, believing those hallway lights were signals about me and my movements. And... I was already hearing the voice, too. Even while I was just standing there in the kitchen stirring a pot of noodles or typing a text message to a friend, I was hearing it, and I was just waiting and watching for the lights to blink. So, no, it wasn't just when I got so incredibly down. It started before all that, for weeks, back when I was still trying to pretend everything was fine.

It feels like I've never felt any other way, even though I know logically that I used to. But it's just been since last summer.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Well, when you say a major life change... I mean, yes. My divorce. He moved out, and suddenly I was just sitting in this half-empty place because he took the good couch and the TV, and I just had this overwhelming sense of... I don't even know, just total failure, I guess. It hurts a lot. \n\nBut it's also the fact that I had to drop out of school because of it. I was doing so well in my classes, finally getting my life on track, and then I just couldn't concentrate. I try pulling the covers up, trying to get warm, trying to force myself back under, but it's impossible. I just lie there, shaking, waiting for the sun to come up, knowing that I have to face another whole day feeling completely drained before it even starts. So, yeah, I wake up way earlier than I should, and falling back asleep is just not an option. It's like my body is terrified of something, but I don't even know what it is.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up shaking and on edge, are those feelings mainly about the divorce and its aftermath?\n\nPatient: No, it’s… I mean, you would think so, right? Because of everything I just said about the paperwork and the empty apartment and how much it hurts. But actually, no. It’s not about the divorce at all in those moments. It’s fear. The people downstairs, mostly. But also some of my ex’s friends. I've been noticing it. It’s the lights in the hallway. I’ll just be sitting in my living room, or lying in bed, and I’ll just... I'll just *think* about going to the store. Like, I won't even pick up my keys, I won't put on my coat or say a single word out loud. I’ll just have the thought in my head, like, 'I should go get some milk.' And right after I think it, the hallway lights blink. I can see the flicker under the gap at the bottom of my front door. It happens right after I think about going out, every single time, like they have some kind of system set up to signal each other that I’m about to move, or like they can actually monitor what I'm planning to do before I even do it. \n\nSo when I wake up at four in the morning shaking like a leaf, it’s because I know they’re out there. I know they’re downstairs, tracking me, and I can't even form a thought about leaving my own apartment without them knowing about it and flashing the lights. I'll get into bed at a normal time, you know, like ten or eleven, hoping that maybe tonight will be the night I actually get some rest, but then my brain just won't shut off. And it's not just thinking about the divorce or the classes I dropped. It's this awful, creeping feeling. I end up getting out of bed, checking the locks on the front door, making sure the deadbolt is turned all the way. Then I'll check the windows. Then I'll go back to bed, but I'll just lie there, staring at the ceiling, listening to every single sound in the building. Every creak of the floorboards upstairs, every car driving by outside, I'm just listening and waiting, feeling so on edge. \n\nSo by the time I actually manage to drift off, which is usually not until two or three in the morning, I'm already exhausted. But then, yes, to answer your question, I wake up way too early. Like, before the sun is even up. It'll be four-thirty or five in the morning, and my eyes just snap open. And I'm not just waking up calmly, I wake up literally shaking. My whole body is just trembling, and my heart is racing like I've been running a marathon in my sleep, though I can never remember what I was dreaming about. And once I'm awake like that, in the pitch dark, shaking and sweating, there is just absolutely no way I can get back to sleep. I try pulling the covers up, trying to get warm, trying to force myself back under, but it's impossible. I just lie there, shaking, waiting for the sun to come up, knowing that I have to face another whole day feeling completely drained before it even starts. So, yeah, I wake up way earlier than I should, and falling back asleep is just not an option. It's like my body is terrified of something, but I don't even know what it is.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up shaking and on edge, are those feelings mainly about the divorce and its aftermath?\n\nPatient: No, it’s… I mean, you would think so, right? Because of everything I just said about the paperwork and the empty apartment and how much it hurts. But actually, no. When I wake up like that, when my heart is just pounding out of my chest and I’m shivering under the covers… it’s not sadness about the marriage ending. It’s not about the divorce at all in those moments. It’s fear. It’s just this absolute, paralyzing terror about what’s happening to me right now, in this building.\n\nBecause—and I know it sounds impossible, I really do, I know how it sounds when I say it out loud—but I’m sure they are tracking me. The people downstairs, mostly. But also some of my ex’s friends. I think they’re coordinating with each other, keeping tabs on exactly when I leave the building, tracking every single move I make. \n\nAnd I wouldn't just say that without a reason, you know? I've been noticing it. It’s the lights in the hallway. I’ll just be sitting in my living room, or lying in bed, and I’ll just... I'll just *think* about going to the store. Like, I won't even pick up my keys, I won't put on my coat or say a single word out loud. I’ll just have the thought in my head, like, 'I should go get some milk.' And right after I think it, the hallway lights blink. I can see the flicker under the gap at the bottom of my front door. It happens right after I think about going out, every single time, like they have some kind of system set up to signal each other that I’m about to move, or like they can actually monitor what I'm planning to do before I even do it. \n\nSo when I wake up at four in the morning shaking like a leaf, it’s because I know they’re out there. I know they’re downstairs, tracking me, and I can't even form a thought about leaving my own apartment without them knowing about it and flashing the lights. It's just so terrifying to be watched like that, to be trapped like that. So, no, it's not the divorce making me shake. It's them.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs that people are tracking you only happened during this period of feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly. I mean, the really heavy, crushing sadness... the part where I couldn't get out of bed or even look at my coursework without completely breaking down... that actually came a bit later. There was this whole stretch of time before the worst of the depression really set in. \n\nDuring that time, I was actually still functioning, at least on the surface, you know? Like, I could still manage to cook sometimes. Nothing elaborate, maybe just boiling some pasta or making a piece of toast so I had something in my stomach, but I was getting up and doing it. And I was still answering my friends when they reached out. I might have just been texting them back to say I was busy or making up excuses for why I couldn't see them, but I was replying, I was still somewhat connected to the world in that way. I wasn't totally shut down yet.\n\nBut the thing is, even during that stretch, even when I was still doing those normal things and pushing through the days... the other stuff was already happening. It had been going on for weeks. I was already watching the gap under the front door, believing those hallway lights were signals about me and my movements. And... I was already hearing the voice, too. Even while I was just standing there in the kitchen stirring a pot of noodles or typing a text message to a friend, I was hearing it, and I was just waiting and watching for the lights to blink. So, no, it wasn't just when I got so incredibly down. It started before all that, for weeks, back when I was still trying to pretend everything was fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's a woman. A woman's voice. And I want to be really clear about this, because I know sometimes people say things like, oh, I have a voice in my head telling me I'm not good enough, like it's just their own thoughts or their inner critic or whatever. But this is not that. It is not inside my head. It sounds exactly—and I mean exactly—as clear as if someone were standing right next to my bed speaking to me. 

The first few times I heard her, I actually sat bolt upright and slapped the lamp on because I was absolutely positive someone had broken into my apartment and was standing right there in the dark. My heart was just hammering in my throat. But there's never anyone there. And it always comes from the exact same spot, too. It always comes from the far corner of my bedroom, over by where I keep the laundry hamper. 

And she just... she says the most awful things. She tells me that I ruined everything. That's what she says, over and over. 'You ruined everything.' And I mean, with the divorce and the empty apartment and having to drop my classes, it's like she knows exactly what hurts the most, exactly what to say to completely break me down. She just repeats that I've destroyed my life, that it's all my fault, that I ruined it all. 

And then she tells me that I have to stay inside. She'll say it so firmly, telling me I shouldn't leave the apartment at all. Which, you know, just makes everything so much worse with the hallway lights flickering and the people downstairs tracking me. She tells me, 'Stay inside, don't go out there, you ruined it all and you need to stay in here.' And she sounds so real, so completely real, like she's just standing right there in that corner watching me under the covers, dictating exactly what I'm supposed to do. It's just... I can't even begin to explain how exhausting it is to have this woman in your room saying these things to you when you're already so terrified and just trying to make it through the night.
